随着美国病例持续激增,丹佛国际机场可能有数千人接触过 麻疹 - UCHealth

Thousands may have been exposed to measles at Denver International Airport as U.S. cases continue to surge - UCHealth
新闻简报2026 年 10 月上旬,一名 麻疹 检测呈阳性的员工多次经过丹佛国际机场,可能使数千人接触到该病毒。科罗拉多州公共卫生和环境部的卫生官员证实了这一接触情况,并强调机场的高客流量是一个风险因素。CDC 报告称,2026 年美国 麻疹 病例激增,确诊病例为 4,588 例,几乎是 2025 年 2,289 例的两倍。科罗拉多州 2026 年确诊病例为 26 例,而 2025 年为 36 例。宾夕法尼亚州有 1,260 例病例,其中 5 例死亡与正在进行的 麻疹 相关。麻疹 具有高度传染性,可导致严重疾病或死亡,尤其是在未接种疫苗的个体中。卫生官员敦促接触者就医,并强调 疫苗接种 对于防止病毒传播的重要性。麻疹病毒 比流感、脊髓灰质炎和埃博拉等其他疾病更容易传播,并且在感染者咳嗽或打喷嚏后可以在空气中停留长达两个小时。
查看新闻五要素
时间
丹佛国际机场的 麻疹 麻疹 病例于 2026 年 10 月上旬报告,该员工于 10 月 3 日至 4 日检测出 麻疹 呈阳性。据 CDC 称,截至 2026 年 10 月 22 日,麻疹 病例激增。
地点与对象
该事件发生在世界上最繁忙的机场之一丹佛国际机场。受影响人群包括未接种疫苗的个人,他们可能在员工经过机场期间接触过 麻疹病毒。
事件
一名后来 麻疹 检测呈阳性的员工于 10 月初多次经过丹佛国际机场,可能使数千人接触到该病毒。科罗拉多州公共卫生和环境部的卫生官员证实了这一点。
规模、影响与风险
截至 2026 年 10 月 22 日,CDC 在美国确认了 4,588 例 麻疹 病例,是 2025 年的两倍。在科罗拉多州,2026 年确诊了 26 例,而 2025 年为 36 例。宾夕法尼亚州报告了 1,260 例病例,其中 5 例死亡与正在进行的 麻疹 相关。麻疹 具有高度传染性,可导致住院或死亡,特别是对于未接种疫苗的个体。
应对、状态与不确定性
卫生官员敦促任何可能接触过感染者并生病的人立即联系医疗服务提供者。他们还强调 疫苗接种 对于预防 麻疹 的重要性,因为感染者咳嗽或打喷嚏后,病毒可以在空气中存活长达两个小时。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Thousands may have been exposed to measles at Denver International Airport as U.S. cases continue to surge - UCHealth

News Brief
In early October 2026, an employee who tested positive for measles traveled through Denver International Airport multiple times, potentially exposing thousands of people to the virus. Health officials from the Colorado Department of Public Health and Environment confirmed this exposure, highlighting the airport's high traffic as a risk factor. The CDC reported a surge in U.S. measles cases in 2026, with 4,588 confirmed cases, nearly double the 2,289 in 2025. In Colorado, 26 cases were confirmed in 2026, compared to 36 in 2025. Pennsylvania had 1,260 cases and five deaths linked to an ongoing outbreak. Measles is highly contagious and can lead to severe illness or death, especially among unvaccinated individuals. Health officials are urging exposed individuals to seek medical attention and emphasizing the importance of vaccination to prevent the spread of the virus. The measles virus spreads more easily than other diseases like flu, polio, and Ebola, and can remain airborne for up to two hours after an infected person coughs or sneezes.

Clinical trials Employees Professionals Media inquiries Financial assistance Contact us News & stories Help me find Doctors Specialties Locations Schedule My Health Connection Log in UCHealth Today All Innovative care Healthy living Stories News Live Colorado Español UCHealth Today > Thousands may have been exposed to measles at Denver International Airport as U.S. cases continue to surge Why is measles so dangerous? Does it spread more easily than the viruses that cause flu, COVID-19, Ebola and polio? And who should get vaccinated to prevent measles? By: Katie Kerwin McCrimmon Yesterday An employee who later tested positive for measles traveled through Denver International Airport, one of the world’s busiest, multiple times in early October, potentially exposing thousands of people to the highly infectious illness. Photo courtesy of Denver International Airport. The number of measles cases across the U.S. this year has surged to nearly double the number of cases in 2025. And a person who was infected with the highly contagious illness traveled three times in early October through Denver International Airport — one of the busiest airports in the world — potentially exposing thousands of people to measles, according to health officials with the Colorado Department of Public Health and Environment. The person is an adult who lives in Jefferson County, Colorado, and health officials did not reveal the person’s vaccination status. The person arrived on a flight at Gate C-50 on Oct. 3 at 12:25 a.m., then rode the busy airport train to the B Concourse and left the airport on an employee shuttle. The person then traveled through Denver International Airport twice the next day. On Oct. 4, the infectious person rode an employee shuttle from a parking lot to Denver International Airport, then departed on a flight from Gate B-48 at 10:57 a.m. The person returned to Denver International Airport at 11:25 p.m. on the night of Oct. 4, arriving at Gate A-18. The employee once again rode the train to Concourse B and took an employee shuttle. Health officials urge anyone who may have been exposed to this person and becomes sick to contact their medical provider immediately. “If you were at (these locations) you may have been exposed,” Colorado health officials wrote in a news release. It’s particularly critical for unvaccinated people to be aware of exposures since measles spreads so easily. Health officials urged anyone who is sick and who might be infected with measles to call their medical provider before showing up at a clinic. Measles cases continue to climb in Colorado and across the U.S. As of October 22, federal health officials at the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) have confirmed 4,588 measles cases across the country in 2026, double the compared 2,289 in all of 2025. About 10% of people who got sick across the U.S. had to be hospitalized. And Pennsylvania health officials have linked 1260 cases and five deaths with an ongoing measles outbreak there. In Colorado, health officials have confirmed 26 measles cases so far in 2026. That compares with 36 cases in all of 2025. Once a measles outbreak starts, it’s very difficult to stop it. That’s because measles is so contagious. It spreads more easily than diseases including COVID-19, flu, polio and Ebola. Along with being extremely infectious, measles is also very dangerous. “Measles can be deadly in children, and many people end up hospitalized. They’re very sick. It can be quite frightening,” said Dr. Michelle Barron. Barron is one of Colorado’s top infectious disease experts and is UCHealth’s senior medical director for infection control and prevention. She also is a professor of medicine and infectious diseases at the University of Colorado Anschutz School of Medicine. These new spikes in measles cases in Colorado and across the U.S. disappoint health officials because the U.S. eradicated measles in 2000. The disease is preventable through a safe and effective vaccine. Why does measles spread so quickly, and where are measles cases highest this year? It’s very difficult to fight measles outbreaks because the disease is so contagious among unvaccinated people. The highest numbers of measles cases through mid-October in the U.S. have occurred in these 12 states, according to CDC and state health department data: Pennsylvania: 1,260 measles cases. South Carolina: 670 measles cases. Utah: 535 measles cases. Wisconsin: 256 measles cases. Texas: 192 measles cases. Virginia: 178 measles cases. Kentucky: 167 measles cases. Ohio: 160 measles cases. Florida: 148 measles cases. New York: 122 measles cases. Arizona: 110 measles cases. California: 55 measles cases. Some of the outbreaks have spread in schools. “When you’re in enclosed spaces for long periods of time, which schools generally are, it’s going to spread,” Barron said. “Everybody’s in classrooms, hallways, bathrooms. Anybody who is breathing the air where an infected person has been could get exposed.” For people who have not been vaccinated, the likelihood of getting measles after exposure is essentially certain, Barron said. That’s because the measles virus has adapted to survive for long periods in the air. “When someone coughs, spits, or sneezes, the virus becomes airborne. Let’s say you are in line with me at the grocery store, and I have measles, and I am coughing or sneezing. The virus can hang out in the air for up to two hours after that, and you can then breathe in the virus,” she said. “If you are not protected by the vaccine, the measles virus then can infect you. CDC researchers estimate that nine of 10 people who lack protection against measles will develop measles infection if exposed to a close contact,’’ Barron said. Her advice is very simple: “Get your vaccine. And if you’re concerned, talk to your provider. They have your best interests at heart.” I thought we had nearly eradicated measles. Why is this dangerous illness coming back? Like other vaccine-preventable diseases, illnesses like measles are on the rise because many parents are not vaccinating their children. Dr. Heather Holmstrom, a family medicine expert at UCHealth Family Medicine Clinic in Boulder, urges all of her patients to get vaccinated. Dr. Heather Holmstrom “We eradicated these diseases, and now they’re coming back,” said Holmstrom, who is also an associate professor of family medicine at the University of Colorado School of Medicine. There are areas in Colorado with poor vaccination rates, like Boulder. And at the same time, these areas have diverse, relatively wealthy populations, with many people traveling around the world. Measles cases are on the rise around the globe. So, increased travel means it’s more likely that Coloradans can bring dangerous diseases home from abroad. Holmstrom spends considerable time educating parents about the importance of vaccines. She also encounters young adults who weren’t vaccinated as children. “I encourage all young adults whose parents didn’t vaccinate them to get vaccinated,” Holmstrom said. Get the facts – Trusted sources on vaccinations Colorado Children’s Immunization Coalition U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention Colorado Department of Public Health and Environment She often has to dispel common myths that have long since been disproven. There is no evidence that vaccines cause autism. Holmstrom and her colleagues have to repeat that message repeatedly and constantly encourage parents to get their babies and children fully vaccinated. “Routine childhood immunizations are really important for protecting every individual child as well as our community,” she said. “People ask me if I’ve vaccinated my own children, and I’m always really open with them. My own children have gotten every routine vaccine ever available.” And she tells them, “Yours should too.” Many doctors have not seen measles since it was eradicated in the U.S. in 2000. But with new outbreaks, more doctors are caring for patients with measles, which appears as a rash, in this case on a person’s back. Photo by Getty Images. Why are measles outbreaks so concerning: a snapshot of the numbers Barron said measles is dangerous because it causes severe medical challenges for those who get it, especially unvaccinated people. Here are some medical complications that can strike people who get measles: At least one in five unvaccinated people in the U.S. who get measles will need to be hospitalized. As many as one in every 20 children who become infected with measles will also get pneumonia, which is the most common cause of death from measles in young children. About 1 in 1,000 children who get measles will develop encephalitis, or swelling of the brain. Encephalitis can lead to very frightening convulsions and can leave a child Deaf or with an intellectual disability. As many as three of every 1,000 children who become infected with measles will die from respiratory and neurologic complications. Measles may cause pregnant women who have not been vaccinated to give birth prematurely or to give birth to a low birth-weight baby. Among people who have gotten sick with measles this year, 94% have been unvaccinated, nearly identical to 2025, when 93% of those with confirmed measles cases also had not received their measles vaccines, according to experts at the CDC. Learn how people with measles can also deal with immunity amnesia. Barron said measles enters the body in a way that also destroys cells that protect our immune systems. The infection essentially destroys what Barron calls our “special forces” that detect and fight infections. That’s why it’s common for people who get measles to also get secondary infections after getting measles. Their bodies don’t have the defenses to fight additional infections. What are the signs and symptoms of measles? Most people get a fever, cough, runny nose or red eyes at first. A rash that looks like red bumps appears on the chest and can appear on the arms and back. Characteristic spots can develop in the mouth. These spots often look like white spots in the back of the throat. Symptoms typically appear 7 to 14 days after exposure. But it may take up to 21 days for an infected person to get sick. A person with measles is contagious for four days before and four days after the rash appears. Signs and symptoms of measles include: Fever, cough, runny nose, and red, watery eyes. The appearance of a rash at the hairline, spreading downward over the body, usually begins 2-4 days after other symptoms. What should you do if you think you might have been exposed to measles? If you think you might have been exposed to measles, it’s vital to stay home so you can avoid spreading the illness. Barron and state health officials are asking parents and individuals who think they might have measles to call ahead before visiting a doctor’s office, urgent care clinic or hospital ER. And be sure to tell health care providers that you might have been exposed to measles. “We really want to minimize the number of other people who are exposed. Even if it turns out that you’re sick with something different, we want to make sure we’re taking precautions. If you think you have measles, you should call ahead. And we would ask that you wear a mask because that helps minimize exposure to others,” Barron said. “Be sure to tell people up front that you’re concerned that you might have measles. We’ll put you in a different room and will expedite the process of having you see a provider.” Symptoms of measles include fever, cough, runny nose, red eyes, and a characteristic rash that usually starts on the face and spreads. What about people who are fully vaccinated for measles? Are they safe to go to school or activities? Barron said people who are fully vaccinated should be fine attending school and visiting public places. “If they’re immunized, they’re safe,” Barron said. “There’s no reason for them (anyone who is fully vaccinated) not to continue with their activities,” she said. Learn why Vitamin A and cod liver oil don’t prevent measles + what it’s like to be sick with this dangerous illness. What if you haven’t been vaccinated for measles? Are you likely to get it? Yes. People who haven’t had the MMR vaccine (which stands for measles, mumps and rubella) are highly likely to get measles if they are exposed to someone with a confirmed case. If an unvaccinated person who is exposed to measles gets the MMR vaccine within 72 hours of exposure to someone with measles, they might be able to prevent a full case of measles. How can you check your MMR vaccination status? Vaccination is highly effective against measles, and people who have previously been vaccinated with the MMR vaccine do not need to receive the vaccine again after exposure to measles. Coloradans can check their/their child’s vaccine status through an online database. Should patients have one or two MMR shots? People who were born prior to 1957 are considered immune. If you were born between 1957 and 1989, you may have only had one shot. You should ask your doctor to either perform a blood test that determines whether your body is protected from the measles virus and whether you need another shot — a booster shot — or simply opt to have an MMR shot without the blood test. If you were born in 1989 or after, you probably have had two shots, but it’s a good idea to verify this. What if a patient was born after 1989 and has had only one shot? See your doctor. Your doctor can perform a blood test to check to see if you need to be protected from the measles virus and whether you need another shot. Or patients can simply opt to have an MMR shot without the blood test. There’s no danger in receiving an additional vaccination. What if patients don’t know if they’ve had the MMR vaccine? Your doctor can order a blood test to check to see if you need to be protected from measles. How long does it take for the vaccination to go into effect so you’re protected from measles? After a person gets the MMR vaccine, it takes about one week for the vaccine to become fully effective. After receiving the first shot, how long do you need to wait to get the second shot? Patients need to wait at least 28 days to get a second shot. Where can I get MMR vaccines? The MMR vaccine is safe and effective at preventing measles (as well as mumps and rubella). Two doses of MMR are approximately 97% effective at preventing measles infection. CDC experts recommend that children receive a first dose of the MMR vaccine at 12 to 15 months of age and a second dose at 4 to 6 years of age. People who receive MMR vaccination according to the U.S. vaccination schedule are usually considered protected for life against measles. The vaccine is widely available in Colorado for free or low cost. Call your medical provider or speak to your doctor during a routine wellness exam for you or your children. The federal Vaccines for Children program provides vaccines at no cost for children from birth through 18 years who are uninsured, underinsured, on Medicaid or Medicaid eligible, and/or Alaskan Native/American Indian. Here’s another snapshot of recent data about measles from the CDC and state health authorities: This year is proving to be tough for the spread of measles, with hundreds of cases already reported across the U.S. South Carolina is having the worst measles outbreak this year, but multiple other states also are seeing high numbers of measles cases. Measles hits children especially hard. Among people with confirmed measles cases in 2025, 26% were under age 5, and an additional 44% were ages 5 to 19. Of those with confirmed measles in 2025, 11% were hospitalized. Measles was officially eradicated in the U.S. in 2000, but after false information about measles vaccines took hold, vaccination rates dropped, and cases have climbed since then. It’s likely that the U.S. now will lose its status as a place where measles has been eradicated. The highest number of cases in recent years prior to 2025 was in 2019, when U.S. health officials reported 1,274 confirmed measles cases nationwide. The U.S. has already surpassed the 2019 high-water mark just two months into 2026. The number of measles cases in the U.S. in 2025 was the highest since 1991, when health officials logged 9,643 cases. That’s when doctors began giving patients two doses of the measles vaccine. Who can I call in Colorado if I’m worried about measles? If you are worried that you may have been exposed to measles and you are experiencing symptoms, immediately notify your health care provider by telephone. Explain that you may have had a possible measles exposure and describe your symptoms. Call a medical provider before physically showing up to minimize the chance of exposure to other people. If you do not have a health care provider, call an urgent care center or emergency department. For additional information about measles, call the free helpline CO-HELP at 303-389-1687 (toll-free: 1-877-462-2911). “Anyone who is experiencing symptoms of measles should stay home unless they need medical treatment,” said Dr. Rachel Herlihy, Colorado State Epidemiologist. “People with signs and symptoms of measles should also not go to child care facilities, school, work, or other public places to avoid exposing others to this very serious and highly contagious disease.” Infectious diseases Measles vaccines Virus prevention You may also like: Flu season hits Colorado early. Positive tests have surged six times higher than last fall. 加拿大如何预防下一个 麻疹 暴发 - Medscape

How Canada Can Prevent the Next Measles Outbreak - Medscape
新闻简报文章讨论了加拿大全国范围内的 麻疹 麻疹 的结束,该活动在 2 个多月没有新增病例后于 2026 年结束。暴发 影响了 10 个省和地区,两年内导致了 6400 多例病例,其中 90% 的病例与未接种疫苗或未知 疫苗接种 状态的个人有关。加拿大公共卫生局 (PHAC) 宣布 暴发 结束,强调需要对未来的 暴发 保持警惕。专家强调保持高 麻疹 发病率对于防止 麻疹 复发的重要性,这可能导致严重的并发症。他们建议医疗保健专业人员和患者验证 疫苗接种 状态并更新协议以保护弱势群体。文章还指出了从美国等邻国引入 麻疹 的持续风险,美国拥有自己的 麻疹。应对措施包括公共卫生举措、政策制定以及改进诊所和医院的感染控制措施。
查看新闻五要素
时间
全国范围内的麻疹 暴发于2026年结束,最后一次相关病例报告是在公告发布前2个多月。该文章发表于2026年10月29日。
地点与对象
暴发 疫情在加拿大 10 个省和地区发生,影响的人群包括未接种疫苗的个人和 疫苗接种 状态未知的人,特别是婴儿、老年人和免疫功能低下的个人等弱势群体。
事件
加拿大公共卫生局(PHAC)宣布全国性麻疹 暴发疫情已正式结束。这是基于过去2个月没有新病例以及暴发的传播期已结束。文章将此事件列为已确认事件。
规模、影响与风险
暴发 在 2 年内导致了 6400 多例病例,其中 90% 的病例发生在未接种疫苗或未知 疫苗接种 状态的个体中。PHAC 指出,暴发 强调了公共卫生系统的弱点以及由于 疫苗接种 率低和可能从邻国重新引入而导致未来 暴发 的风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
PHAC 已经结束了 暴发,现在正致力于通过 疫苗接种 努力预防未来的 暴发。专家建议保持高度警惕,更新方案,并确保医护人员和患者了解他们的 疫苗接种 状态。应对措施正在进行中,涉及公共卫生举措和政策制定。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

How Canada Can Prevent the Next Measles Outbreak - Medscape

News Brief
The article discusses the conclusion of a nationwide measles outbreak in Canada, which ended in 2026 after more than 2 months without new cases. The outbreak, which affected 10 provinces and territories, resulted in over 6400 cases over 2 years, with 90% of cases linked to unvaccinated or unknown vaccination status individuals. Public Health Agency of Canada (PHAC) declared the outbreak over, emphasizing the need for vigilance against future outbreaks. Experts highlight the importance of maintaining high vaccination rates to prevent the resurgence of measles, which can lead to severe complications. They recommend healthcare professionals and patients to verify vaccination status and update protocols to protect vulnerable groups. The article also notes the ongoing risk of measles introduction from neighboring countries like the United States, which has its own outbreak. The response includes public health initiatives, policy development, and improved infection control measures in clinics and hospitals.
Original source text (English)
Clinical Hub News & Perspective Tools & Reference CME/CE More EN RegisterLog In Ask AI Search AI Mode Medscape News Canada How Canada Can Prevent the Next Measles Outbreak Carolyn Crist October 29, 2026 10 Add to Email Alerts Jump to Key Points The nationwide measles outbreak — which resulted in more than 6400 cases across 10 provinces and territories in 2 years — has officially ended, according to the Public Health Agency of Canada (PHAC). The agency made its announcement after more than 2 months had passed since the last associated case was reported. Infectious diseases experts in Canada are cautiously optimistic but recommend vigilance for future outbreaks. “It’s helpful and a step in the right direction that this outbreak is now declared finished, but this is not the finish line. This is the starting gate,” Isaac Bogoch, MD, professor of medicine at the University of Toronto and infectious diseases specialist at Toronto General Hospital, told Medscape News Canada. “Measles represents the canary in the coal mine. It shows the chinks in the armor of public health systems,” he said. “You have to think about other vaccine-preventable diseases — such as influenza, polio, and tuberculosis — and I’m not sure if we’ve really taken the necessary steps to prevent subsequent outbreaks.” Measles Outbreak Response Measles was first eliminated in Canada in 1998. During the nearly 30 year-period when measles was considered eliminated (1998-2024), the country reported an average of 91 cases annually, with 0-752 each year. Following sustained transmission of the same virus strain for more than 1 year, PHAC announced in November 2025 that the country’s measles elimination status had been lost. SUGGESTED FOR YOU Isaac Bogoch, MD “We never should have been in a position where a high-income country with a sound healthcare and public health system should lose our measles elimination status, but here we are,” said Bogoch. “The drivers are similar here and worldwide — we’ve been chipping away at public health initiatives through a lack of funding and a lack of trust.” The multijurisdictional outbreak began in New Brunswick with a patient who was exposed to measles outside Canada. Additional cases with symptom onset after October 24, 2024, were considered part of the outbreak if the measles virus sequence matched that of the first case or if epidemiologic evidence (such as direct contact with an outbreak-related case) supported a connection. As of October 26, 1126 measles cases have been reported so far this year, according to Canada’s measles and rubella weekly monitoring report. Two new cases unrelated to the multijurisdictional outbreak were reported in Quebec during October 11-17. Seven measles cases are being monitored in British Columbia, Ontario, and Quebec. During the past 2 years, more than 90% of cases occurred among people who were unvaccinated or had an unknown vaccination status. “In the big picture of what we can do as healthcare professionals, the fundamental root of the issue is vaccination. We originally achieved measles elimination by having almost everyone vaccinated across Canada,” Matthew Muller, MD, PhD, medical director of infection prevention and control and an infectious diseases physician at St. Michael’s Hospital and Unity Health Toronto, told Medscape News Canada. “If we can achieve herd immunity with 95% of people being vaccinated, there’s no need for anyone to get measles, including vulnerable people who can’t be vaccinated,” he said. “I want healthcare workers to remember that measles isn’t just a rash and a fever — it can be a severe disease that leads to severe complications, and I don’t want to go back to seeing those develop.” Two doses of a measles-containing vaccine can provide strong protection against infection. Canadians — including healthcare professionals — should check with their provider or local public health unit to verify their measles vaccination status, PHAC said. Matthew Muller, MD, PhD “With vaccination, if the percentage falls below herd immunity, it’s like a forest getting too dry, and then a spark can ignite a wildfire,” Muller said. “If we have big areas in Canada where people aren’t vaccinated, then one case can trigger the fire that leads to a widespread outbreak.” Muller also serves as chair in the Ontario Provincial Infectious Diseases Advisory Committee on Infection Prevention and Control and has helped with guideline and policy development around infection prevention in Ontario hospitals. He and his colleagues have talked about the importance of vaccination — and of having conversations with patients about their vaccination status. “It’s important to have open and frank conversations, not shying away or making assumptions about why someone may not be vaccinated,” he said. “Listen to understand their concerns and then help debunk myths and be a resource for them.” Future Outbreak Preparation Along with vaccination conversations, healthcare providers in hospitals and community settings can remain prepared by looking for measles symptoms and handling suspected cases appropriately. “While it’s great that this current outbreak is under control, there will continue to be introduction of measles into the population, especially with our closest geographic neighbor [the United States] having an ongoing outbreak in various states. We need to keep our index of suspicion high for measles when presented with compatible symptoms,” Susy Hota, MD, medical director for infection prevention and control and division head of infectious diseases at University Health Network in Toronto, told Medscape News Canada. Clinics and ambulatory settings, in particular, may feel more vulnerable and less prepared when handling unannounced cases that walk in the door, she noted. As part of an infection control policy, clinics can implement protocols that encourage patients and staff to know their vaccination status, provide time for staff to receive vaccinations, and design clinic intake to protect vulnerable groups from infection. Susy Hota, MD For instance, when confirming patient appointments, staff scripts should ask about broad respiratory symptoms, as well as measles-specific signs, such as a new rash. If new symptoms are disclosed at sign-in, staff can isolate suspected cases and put systems in place to care for pregnant patients, those with weakened immune systems, and vulnerable patients who may experience severe symptoms, such as infants or older adults. Updating these protocols now can help when an unexpected case occurs in the future, Hota said. “While reviewing a previous case, we found that measles hadn’t been recognized as part of the initial differential review, and the patient traveled from that clinic area to the lab for blood collection, which was adjacent to the preadmission area and led to massive exposure that was difficult to track,” she said. “When you plan ahead, include measles as a possible diagnosis, and follow protocols, you can isolate the patient, wear the appropriate protective gear, obtain bloodwork in a safe way, and mitigate the risks of spread.” Bogoch, Muller, and Hota reported having no relevant financial relationships. Carolyn Crist is a health and medical journalist who reports on the latest studies for Medscape, MDedge, and WebMD. View five news elements
Time
The nationwide measles outbreak ended in 2026, with the last associated case reported more than 2 months prior to the announcement. The article was published on October 29, 2026.
Location and population
The outbreak occurred across 10 provinces and territories in Canada, affecting a population that included unvaccinated individuals and those with unknown vaccination status, particularly among vulnerable groups such as infants, older adults, and immunocompromised individuals.
Event
The Public Health Agency of Canada (PHAC) declared the nationwide measles outbreak officially ended. This was based on the absence of new cases for over 2 months and the conclusion of the outbreak's transmission period. The article reports this as a confirmed event.
Scale, impact and risk
The outbreak resulted in more than 6400 cases over 2 years, with 90% of cases occurring among unvaccinated or unknown vaccination status individuals. PHAC noted that the outbreak highlighted weaknesses in public health systems and the risk of future outbreaks due to low vaccination rates and potential reintroduction from neighboring countries.
Response, status and uncertainty
PHAC has concluded the outbreak and is now focusing on preventing future outbreaks through vaccination efforts. Experts recommend maintaining high vigilance, updating protocols, and ensuring healthcare workers and patients are aware of their vaccination status. The response is ongoing and involves public health initiatives and policy development.

US measles cases top 4,800 as the disease surges in multiple states - CIDRAP
新闻简报截至 2026 年 10 月 30 日,美国已报告超过 4,800 例 麻疹 病例,宾夕法尼亚州和威斯康星州分别记录了 88 例和 82 例新感染病例。病例激增引发了人们对 麻疹 准确度和疾病传播的担忧,因为一项研究发现 7% 的 麻疹 病例被误诊。CIDRAP 指出,由于 疫苗接种 的阻力越来越大,卫生专业人员正在努力管理 暴发。目前情况仍在调查中,呼吁加强公共卫生准备并信任 疫苗接种 的努力。该报告强调了解决 疫苗 犹豫不决和确保有效遏制策略方面的更广泛挑战。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

US measles cases top 4,800 as the disease surges in multiple states - CIDRAP

News Brief
As of October 30, 2026, the US has reported over 4,800 measles cases, with Pennsylvania and Wisconsin each recording 88 and 82 new infections, respectively. The surge in cases has raised concerns about accurate diagnosis and disease spread, as a study found 7% of measles cases are misdiagnosed. CIDRAP notes that health professionals are struggling to manage the outbreaks amid growing pushback on vaccinations. The situation remains under investigation, with calls for improved public health preparedness and trust in vaccination efforts. The report highlights the broader challenges in addressing vaccine hesitancy and ensuring effective containment strategies.

与 传播 相关的大型 麻疹 暴发 在美洲和加拿大失去 麻疹 消除状态的影响:描述性 队列研究 研究

Impact of a large measles outbreak associated with transmission in the Americas and Canada’s loss of measles elimination status: descriptive cohort study
新闻简报2024年10月至2025年10月在加拿大安大略省发生的一场大型麻疹 麻疹导致加拿大麻疹失去淘汰资格。暴发 涉及 2377 例病例,其中 2064 例确诊,313 例疑似病例,与历史上疫苗接种不足的社区有关。大多数病例 (90.3%) 发生在这些社区中,普通人群中的 跨物种溢出 病例有限。暴发 菌株被命名为 MVs/Ontario.CAN/47.24,被发现已在国际上传播到美国、墨西哥和荷兰。麻疹 与 166 例住院、13 例入住 ICU、51 例妊娠期间感染、10 例先天性 麻疹 感染、1 例脑炎和 1 例婴儿死亡相关。采取了公共卫生措施,包括病例隔离和暴露后预防。麻疹 强调了 麻疹 在处于消除状态的国家重新建立的风险,并强调社区参与对于提高 疫苗接种 率和防止未来 麻疹 的重要性。
查看新闻五要素
时间
暴发期间为2024年10月28日至2025年10月6日的流行病学周。报告发布日期为2026年10月27日。
地点与对象
暴发 发生在加拿大安大略省,涉及历史上疫苗接种不足的社区。它还将 跨物种溢出 限制在更广泛的人群中,并传播到其他国家,包括美国、墨西哥和荷兰。
事件
据报道,大量 麻疹 麻疹 导致加拿大失去 麻疹 淘汰状态。暴发 被确认并描述为描述性 队列研究 研究。
规模、影响与风险
暴发 包括 49 周内的 2377 例病例(2064 例确诊病例,313 例疑似病例)。它与 166 例住院、13 例入住 ICU、51 例妊娠期间感染、10 例先天性 麻疹 感染、1 例脑炎和 1 例婴儿死亡有关。暴发 菌株被鉴定为新的 N450 独特序列,并指定为 WHO 命名菌株 (MVs/Ontario.CAN/47.24)。
应对、状态与不确定性
暴发 与公共卫生应对措施相关,包括病例隔离、接触者追踪和暴露后预防。麻疹病毒 序列的系统发育分析用于评估国际传播。暴发 于 2024 年 10 月在加拿大新不伦瑞克省的一场婚礼上曝光后开始,并蔓延到其他省份和国家。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Impact of a large measles outbreak associated with transmission in the Americas and Canada’s loss of measles elimination status: descriptive cohort study

News Brief
A large measles outbreak in Ontario, Canada, from October 2024 to October 2025 led to the loss of Canada's measles elimination status. The outbreak involved 2377 cases, with 2064 confirmed and 313 probable, and was linked to historically under-vaccinated communities. Most cases (90.3%) occurred among these communities, with limited spillover into the general population. The outbreak strain, designated as MVs/Ontario.CAN/47.24, was found to have spread internationally to the United States, Mexico, and the Netherlands. The outbreak was associated with 166 hospital admissions, 13 ICU admissions, 51 infections during pregnancy, 10 congenital measles infections, one case of encephalitis, and one infant death. Public health measures, including case isolation and post-exposure prophylaxis, were implemented. The outbreak highlights the risk of measles re-establishment in countries with elimination status and underscores the importance of community engagement to improve vaccination rates and prevent future outbreaks.
Original source text (English)
Sarah E Wilson , public health physician 1 2 3 4 , Gillian H Lim , epidemiologist 1 , Mehdi Aloosh , medical officer of health 5 6 , Adelle Chang On , associate medical officer of health 7 , Cara-Lee Coghill , public health nurse 8 , Tara Harris , program manager 1 , Maan Hasso , microbiologist 1 9 , Miriam Klassen , medical officer of health 10 , Christine Navarro , public health physician 1 2 3 , Eleanor Paget , nurse consultant 1 , Kaitlin Patterson , epidemiologist 11 , Marina I Salvadori , paediatric infectious disease physician 11 12 , Ninh Tran , medical officer of health 8 , John Pathobiology, Faculty of Medicine, University of Toronto, Toronto, Ontario, Canada 10 Huron Perth Public Health, Stratford, Ontario, Canada 11 Centre for Immunization Surveillance and Programs, Public Health Agency of Canada, Ottawa, Ontario, Canada 12 Department of Pediatrics, McGill University, Montreal, Quebec, Canada 13 Algoma Public Health, Sault Ste Marie, Ontario, Canada 14 Northern Ontario School of Medicine, Sudbury, Ontario, Canada 15 Office of the Chief Medical Officer of Health, Ontario Ministry of Health, Toronto, Ontario, Canada 16 Measles, Mumps and Rubella Section, National Microbiology Laboratory Branch, Public Health Agency of Canada, Winnipeg, Manitoba, Canada Correspondence to: S Wilson ( sarah.wilson@oahpp.ca ) Abstract Objective To describe the epidemiological characteristics, public health response, and evidence of international spread of a large measles outbreak that contributed to the loss of Canada’s measles elimination status. Design Descriptive analysis of the cohort of measles outbreak cases reported to Ontario’s public health surveillance system during the outbreak period: epidemiological week of 28 October 2024 to 6 October 2025. Participants Individuals meeting the outbreak case definition for confirmed or probable measles infection during the outbreak period. Exposure Exposure to measles virus associated with the Ontario outbreak and related transmission chains. Main outcome measures Demographic characteristics, vaccination status, admission to hospital, complications, pregnancy associated outcomes, mortality, and evidence of international spread based on measles virus genotyping and sequencing. Cases were identified using an outbreak-specific case definition incorporating epidemiological, clinical, laboratory, and viral sequencing criteria. Phylogenetic analysis of measles virus sequences was used to assess evidence of onwards spread to other countries. Results The outbreak consisted of 2377 cases (2064 confirmed and 313 probable) over 49 weeks and contributed to the loss of Canada’s measles elimination status. Most cases (2146/2377, 90.3%) occurred among members of historically under-vaccinated communities, with limited spillover into the broader population. Among the 2377 cases, approximately three quarters of cases (n=1736, 73.0%) occurred in individuals aged 19 years or younger, 2121 (89.2%) were unvaccinated, and 97 (4.1%) had unknown vaccination status. The outbreak was associated with 166 (7%) admissions to hospital, 13 (<1%) admissions to intensive care unit, 51 (2.1%) infections during pregnancy, 10 (<1%) congenital measles infections, one (<1%) case of encephalitis, and one (<1%) infant death. Sequencing identified a novel N450 distinct sequence identifier that was subsequently detected in countries including the United States of America, Mexico, and the Netherlands. The outbreak strain was designated a World Health Organization named strain (MVs/Ontario.CAN/47.24). Conclusions These findings highlight the risk of measles re-establishment in countries that have attained elimination and underscore the importance of culturally appropriate community engagement to build trust in immunisation and prevent future outbreaks. This outbreak shows the fragility of measles elimination in the presence of long standing immunity gaps within interconnected communities across the Americas and the population-level protection that limited broader community transmission. Introduction Measles is a highly infectious respiratory virus. Its high transmissibility reflects short and long range transmission of infectious respiratory particles through the air and a period of communicability extending from four days before until four days after rash onset. 1 Consequently, susceptible contacts are frequently exposed to the virus before cases are recognised and public health control measures, including case isolation and post-exposure prophylaxis, can be effectively implemented. Measles can cause severe complications, admission to hospital, and death, particularly among infants, pregnant individuals, and people who are immunocompromised. 1 Beyond its direct health effects, measles is associated with substantial social and economic burdens on individuals and families. In a patient-based survey, acute measles infection was associated with a mean illness duration of 13.8 days, 9.6 days away from school or work, and substantial reductions in health related quality of life. 2 Despite the availability of a safe and highly effective vaccine, measles outbreaks have re-emerged in several countries that had previously achieved elimination. 3 Canada attained measles elimination status in 1998 after the introduction of a second dose of measles-containing vaccine and a national catch-up programme for school-aged children. 4 5 High uptake of the two dose schedule led to a marked decline in measles incidence. In the province of Ontario, more than 5000 cases were reported in 1991, 6 compared with 101 cases reported between 2013 and 2023. 7 In November 2025, the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO) determined that Canada lost its measles elimination status due to ongoing domestic transmission associated with a multijurisdictional measles outbreak. 8 The outbreak began in October 2024, after exposure to measles at a wedding in New Brunswick, Canada, followed by secondary cases among attendees in the provinces of New Brunswick and Ontario. 7 Ontario is home to several diverse ethno-religious and cultural groups with vaccine coverage that has historically been low due to a combination of structural barriers, historical experience with health systems, and community specific perspectives on vaccination. These groups, which include some Mennonite, Amish, and Netherlands Reformed communities, have close social networks spanning Canada, the United States of America (USA), Mexico, and other countries that create opportunities for cross-border transmission. 9 10 11 We describe the epidemiological and genomic characteristics of the Ontario measles outbreak, which formed part of a broader multijurisdictional outbreak involving other Canadian provinces, 12 the US, Mexico and several other countries across the Americas. 13 14 We also summarise the public health response and some of the unique challenges posed by this outbreak, and their implications for re-establishing measles elimination, which are relevant for other countries at risk of losing elimination status. Methods Measles case identification Measles is reportable in Ontario under the Health Protection and Promotion Act 15 with routine surveillance, case definitions, and a provincial protocol for case and contact management. 16 Measles is classically characterised by fever, cough, coryza, conjunctivitis, and rash, although the rash may be absent or atypical in people with immunocompromising conditions, some neonates with congenital infection, and in breakthrough cases in previously vaccinated individuals. To support case finding activities, an outbreak-specific case definition was established, 17 allowing inclusion of clinically compatible cases with relevant epidemiological linkages. On 10 June 2025, the outbreak case definition was updated to incorporate sequencing information, classifying laboratory-confirmed cases as outbreak-related if viral sequences matched defined distinct sequence IDs (DSId), 18 even in the absence of direct epidemiological linkage; this change was also applied retrospectively. Laboratory diagnosis of measles and virus sequencing Measles virus RNA was detected using a real-time reverse transcription polymerase chain reaction (RT-PCR) assay. Diagnostic testing was conducted at Public Health Ontario regional laboratories with RT-PCR testing introduced at one non-Public Health Ontario laboratory on 16 June 2025. All specimens positive for measles underwent secondary RT-PCR testing to differentiate wild-type virus from vaccine strain and all wild-type virus detections were referred to the National Microbiology Laboratory for genotyping and sequencing (further details outlined in the eMethods in the supplementary appendix). 19 20 21 To increase the uptake of laboratory testing, patient self-collection of specimens was accepted, facilitated by local public health agencies, and evaluated as part of quality assurance activities. Public health case investigation All reported cases were investigated by local public health agencies, with demographic, clinical, exposure, vaccination, and laboratory data entered into Ontario’s public health surveillance system. Vaccination status was determined using doses of measles-containing vaccine administered at least 14 days before symptom onset. Local public health agency staff also determined if suspect cases were members of historically under-vaccinated communities. For cases in pregnant women, additional information was collected, including pregnancy and infant outcomes. Confirmed congenital measles cases were identified using a case definition provided by the Public Health Agency of Canada. 12 For the one report of death, a case conference approach, with clinicians involved in direct patient care, determined the extent to which measles contributed to the cause of death. Data source for vaccine coverage and doses administered We used Panorama, Ontario’s public health immunisation repository, to calculate two dose measles-containing vaccine coverage for the school year preceding the outbreak. We also used the repository to determine the number of doses administered directly by local public health agency staff through the outbreak vaccination strategy. Although Panorama captures vaccines administered by local public health staff during outbreak responses, most vaccines are administered in primary care and are added retrospectively based on records collected by local public health agencies under the Immunization of School Pupils Act. The completeness of these data is known to have been affected by a pause in record-collection processes during the covid-19 pandemic, particularly for younger children, and by incomplete capture of vaccinations among children attending private religious schools. 22 23 Recent trends in coverage of measles-containing vaccine at the provincial and local public health agency levels were obtained from Public Health Ontario’s immunisation data tool, 24 which uses Panorama as its data source and is subject to the same limitations. Analysis and reporting The cohort of measles outbreak cases was analysed descriptively using SAS Enterprise Guide 8.2. We calculated two dose coverage of measles-containing vaccine among 17 year olds, a group less likely to have been affected by pandemic-related disruptions in vaccine record collection and measles outbreak incidence rate (for all ages) at the small area level. We calculated and mapped these measures by forward sortation area using the 2021 Canadian Census. Phylogenetic analysis of N450 sequences was performed to describe the Ontario outbreak strain and assess the extent to which it has been implicated in measles activity outside of Canada (further details outlined in the eMethods in the supplementary appendix). Patient and public involvement Local community members and faith leaders were engaged in the outbreak response, including in the development of public health messaging. Results Measles outbreak size and duration The Ontario measles outbreak resulted in a total of 2377 reported (2064 confirmed and 313 probable) cases ( table 1 , figure 1 ). The outbreak began during the epidemiological week of 28 October 2024, and was declared over on 6 October 2025, in accordance with the Public Health Agency of Canada’s criteria for measles outbreak closure. 1 During the same 49 week period, 50 measles cases not associated with the outbreak were reported in Ontario. Measles cases imported from travel have occurred sporadically in Ontario since elimination in 1998, typically in urban or suburban areas. 25 By contrast, among the five local public health agencies with the highest outbreak case incidence, four had not reported a case of measles in over 25 years (eTable 1, supplementary appendix). All five local public health agencies are located in rural and semi-rural communities in the province and four of the five share geographical boundaries in southwestern Ontario. Prior to the onset of the outbreak, recorded two dose measles vaccine coverage in Ontario was 76.8% among 7 year olds and 91.8% among 17 year olds, based on local public health agency vaccination records. 24 Figure 2 presents maps of measles vaccine coverage at the onset of the outbreak and measles outbreak incidence at the conclusion of the outbreak. Table 1 Characteristics of measles outbreak cases Fig 1 Outbreak and non-outbreak measles cases in Ontario, Canada: 28 October 2024 to 6 October 2025 Fig 2 Two dose measles vaccine coverage among 17-year-olds in 2023-24 (A) and 2024-25 measles outbreak incidence (B), by forward sortation area in Ontario, Canada. Forward sortation areas are geographical areas defined by the first three characters of Canadian postal codes. Because forward sortation area boundaries reflect population distribution, they are substantially larger in sparsely populated regions of Northern Ontario than in Southern Ontario. Consequently, maps based on forward sortation areas may not accurately represent the geographical extent of measles transmission risk or areas of low vaccination coverage The Ontario outbreak began following the exposure of several Ontario residents to a guest from Thailand, 26 during a wedding in New Brunswick in October 2024. 7 Public Health Ontario was notified by New Brunswick public health officials and alerted six local public health agencies in southwestern Ontario to initiate contact tracing and follow-up investigations. Secondary cases were identified among wedding attendees and later among household contacts who had not attended the event. Between 28 October 2024 and 1 January 2025, 37 measles outbreak cases were reported to three local public health agencies in southwestern Ontario ( figure 1 ) but only one case was confirmed by laboratory testing. Limited uptake of diagnostic testing during this period reflected both community members’ reluctance to be tested and, to a lesser extent, concerns among primary care providers regarding transmission risk during specimen collection. Source of measles acquisition After a period of minimal case reporting in December 2024, which was likely indicative of ongoing but undetected transmission, new cases began to rapidly emerge in January 2025. Multiple cases were associated with exposures in community and healthcare settings, resulting in well documented chains of transmission. In one instance, a single case was linked to 20 subsequent cases (eight secondary and 12 tertiary cases) over five weeks. Case investigations also identified transmission during social gatherings within under-vaccinated communities with close social ties across the province. Over time, the outbreak expanded to involve additional historically under-vaccinated communities across the province (eFigure 1, supplementary appendix), with reported cases peaking in the week of 28 April 2025 ( figure 1 ). Overall, 2146 (90.3%) of 2377 outbreak cases occurred among members of historically under-vaccinated communities including Mennonite, Amish, and Netherlands Reformed communities ( table 1 ). As the outbreak progressed, identifying specific sources of exposure became increasingly difficult due to widespread transmission within the most affected communities. Among all 2377 outbreak cases, 74.4% (n=1768) were associated with a household with more than one case; among households with multiple cases, the median was five cases per household (range 2-11). Schools, including school buses, (14.9% (n=355)) and community gatherings (8.1% (n=192)) were reported as likely sources of acquisition. Eight cases occurred among migrant agricultural workers (referred to as international agricultural workers in Ontario) or their household members in southwestern Ontario. Over time, related measles outbreaks emerged in other regions of Canada, the USA, and Mexico, 13 14 frequent travel and social connections between affected communities meant that some cases had multiple plausible exposures across jurisdictions, complicating efforts to determine where transmission had occurred. Outbreak case characteristics Among the 2377 outbreak cases, 73.0% occurred in individuals 19 years or younger, including 583 (24.5%) cases in children under 5 years old ( table 1 )....

Ontario recorded 2,377 measles cases — News-Medical - UA.NEWS

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Ontario recorded 2,377 measles cases — News-Medical - UA.NEWS

News Brief
Ontario, Canada, experienced a measles outbreak lasting 49 weeks, from October 28, 2024, to October 6, 2025, with 2,377 cases reported. Of these, 2,064 were confirmed and 313 were probable. Most cases occurred in communities with historically low vaccination rates across Canada, the United States, Mexico, and other countries, with 73% of cases involving individuals aged 19 or younger. The outbreak led to 166 hospitalizations, including 13 intensive care unit admissions, and resulted in 51 cases of infection during pregnancy, 10 cases of congenital measles, one case of encephalitis, and one infant death. Canada lost its measles elimination status in November 2025 due to this outbreak. Researchers noted that the actual number of cases and complications might be higher than recorded and emphasized the importance of strengthening immunization systems, increasing vaccination coverage, and culturally adapted engagement to prevent future outbreaks.
Original source text (English)
News Health Ontario recorded 2,377 measles cases — News-Medical UA.NEWS 29 October 2026 05:36 In Ontario, Canada, a measles outbreak lasted 49 weeks — from October 28, 2024, to October 6, 2025 — and included 2,377 cases. It contributed to Canada losing its measles elimination status in November 2025, News-Medical reports , citing a study published in The BMJ. Most cases were among unvaccinated people Researchers analyzed data from Ontario's surveillance system. Of the 2,377 cases, 2,064 were confirmed and 313 were probable. About 90% of cases occurred among members of communities with historically low vaccination rates that have close social ties in Canada, the United States, Mexico, and other countries. Spread beyond these communities was limited. Of all patients, 73% were aged 19 years or younger. A total of 2,121 patients, or 89% of the total, were unvaccinated; for another 97 people, or 4%, vaccination status was unknown. Canada acquired measles-free status in 1998 after introducing a two-dose vaccination schedule and a national catch-up vaccination program for schoolchildren. More current news is available on the UA.News Telegram channel Telegram . Hospitalizations and strain spread A total of 166 hospitalizations were linked to this outbreak, including 13 admissions to intensive care units. There were also 51 cases of infection during pregnancy, 10 cases of congenital measles, one case of encephalitis, and the death of one infant. Laboratory studies identified a distinct measles virus strain that was later recorded in other parts of Canada, as well as in the United States, Mexico, and the Netherlands. Researchers cautioned that the actual number of cases and complications may be higher than recorded in surveillance data. In their view, preventing new outbreaks requires strengthening immunization systems, increasing vaccination coverage, and culturally adapted engagement with communities to build trust in vaccination. 随着 麻疹、暴发 在美国扩散,地图与图表显示 2026 年病例数创历史新高——《哥伦比亚广播公司新闻》

As measles outbreaks grow in the U.S., maps and charts show a record number of cases in 2026 - CBS News
新闻简报2026年,美国麻疹病例大幅增加,截至10月确诊病例已超过4,500例,超过上一年的2,200多例总数。麻疹 已蔓延到多个州,引发了人们对该国可能失去自 2000 年以来的 麻疹 消除状态的担忧。宾夕法尼亚州报告了 2026 年发生的两起与 麻疹 相关的死亡事件,均发生在兰卡斯特县,后来还有几起与 麻疹 有关的死亡事件。这些病例涉及未接种疫苗的个体,凸显了低 疫苗接种 率在该疾病复发中的作用。CDC 指出,2025-2026 学年,幼儿园儿童的 疫苗接种 率小幅下降至 92.4%,低于上一年的 92.5%,低于社区免疫所需的 95% 临界值。卫生官员和疾病预防控制中心正在积极监测情况,强调 疫苗接种 对于防止进一步传播和保护公众健康的重要性。

时间
到2026年7月中旬,麻疹案件数量已超过2025年全年。到2026年10月,案件数量突破4500起。
地点与对象
美国,暴发 在多个州蔓延。大多数病例发生在未接种疫苗的个体中,特别是在 疫苗接种 率较低的社区。
事件
麻疹 暴发 2026年在美国出现增长,确诊病例数创历史新高。宾夕法尼亚州报告称,2026 年有两例与 麻疹 有关的死亡,均发生在兰开斯特县,后来又有几例死亡与 麻疹 有关。
规模、影响与风险
截至 2026 年 10 月,美国已记录了 4,500 多例 麻疹 确诊病例,超过 2025 年的 2,200 多例总数。暴发 引起了人们对美国自 2000 年以来失去 麻疹 消除状态的担忧。CDC 数据显示 疫苗接种 比率下降,2025-2026 学年只有 92.4% 的幼儿园儿童接受 麻疹-腮腺炎-风疹 疫苗,低于上年为92.5%。
应对、状态与不确定性
卫生官员正在全国范围内追踪 麻疹 病例。CDC强调疫苗接种对于维持社区免疫力的重要性。随着州卫生部门和疾病预防控制中心发布新数据,哥伦比亚广播公司新闻数据团队一直在监测确诊病例。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

As measles outbreaks grow in the U.S., maps and charts show a record number of cases in 2026 - CBS News

News Brief
In 2026, the U.S. has experienced a significant rise in measles cases, with over 4,500 confirmed by October, surpassing the previous year's total of more than 2,200. Outbreaks have spread across multiple states, raising concerns about the country potentially losing its measles elimination status since 2000. Pennsylvania reported two measles-related deaths in 2026, both in Lancaster County, and several more were later linked to the outbreak. These cases involved unvaccinated individuals, highlighting the role of low vaccination rates in the resurgence of the disease. The CDC notes that vaccination rates for kindergartners dropped slightly to 92.4% in the 2025-2026 school year, down from 92.5% the previous year, and below the critical 95% threshold needed for community immunity. Health officials and the CDC are actively monitoring the situation, emphasizing the importance of vaccination to prevent further spread and protect public health.

Latest U.S. Iran War Stranded Cruise World Politics Entertainment HealthWatch MoneyWatch Crime Space Sports Brand Studio The Free Press Local News Atlanta Baltimore Bay Area Boston Chicago Colorado Detroit Los Angeles Miami Minnesota New York Philadelphia Pittsburgh Sacramento Texas Live CBS News 24/7 Baltimore Bay Area Boston Chicago Colorado Detroit Los Angeles Miami Minnesota New York Philadelphia Pittsburgh Sacramento Texas 48 Hours 60 Minutes Shows 48 Hours 60 Minutes CBS Evening News CBS Mornings CBS Morning News CBS Reports CBS Saturday Morning The Daily Report The Dish Face the Nation Sunday Morning The Takeout Things That Matter The Uplift CBS News Investigates CBS News Confirmed Podcasts Newsletters Download Our App CBS News Team Executive Team Brand Studio Paramount+ Join Our Talent Community RSS Feeds Watch CBS News HealthWatch As measles outbreaks grow in the U.S., maps and charts show a record number of cases in 2026 By Grace Manthey Grace Manthey Senior Visual Data Journalist, Data Team Coordinator Grace Manthey is the senior visual data journalist and data team coordinator for CBS News and Stations. She is an Emmy-winning journalist and visual storyteller with a passion for telling stories that matter. Read Full Bio Grace Manthey Updated on: October 29, 2026 / 2:04 PM EDT / CBS News Add CBS News on Google So far in 2026, the U.S. has recorded the most measles cases in more than three decades as outbreaks spread in multiple states, according to data from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. By mid-July, the number had already surpassed all of 2025, which had been the worst year in recent history with more than 2,200 confirmed cases. By October, the number of cases topped 4,500. Ongoing outbreaks have put the U.S. at risk of losing its official measles elimination status, which it has held since 2000. In August, health officials in Pennsylvania reported the state's first two deaths connected to measles in 35 years, both in Lancaster County. Both of the individuals were not vaccinated against the virus, state health officials said. Several more deaths associated with measles were later reported as the state's outbreak surged. Measles is one of the most contagious infectious diseases. In some cases, it can cause severe infections in the lungs and brain that can lead to cognitive issues, deafness or death. But it can be prevented by vaccination, which medical research has proven to be safe and highly effective. The CBS News data team has tracked confirmed measles cases nationwide as new data is released by state health departments and the CDC. Out of the 2,289 confirmed cases last year, more than 750 were connected to an outbreak in West Texas that led to the deaths of two children. Prior to 2025, the highest number of confirmed cases in the U.S. in recent years was 1,274 in 2019, driven by outbreaks in New York, California and Washington state. Most years, the total was much lower. Health experts point to lower vaccination rates as the main reason for increases in preventable infectious diseases like measles. The vast majority of cases have been in people who were unvaccinated, and outbreaks have spread in communities with lower vaccination rates. CDC data shows about 92.4% of kindergartners in the U.S. got their measles-mumps-rubella shots in the 2025-2026 school year, down slightly from 92.5% the previous year. The figure has declined from 95.2% during the 2019-2020 school year — a critical threshold to keep people safe. Only 10 states hit the 95% threshold last year. "When more than 95% of people in a community are vaccinated, most people are protected through community immunity (herd immunity)," the CDC states. "I think that parents, for most kids, especially for the measles vaccines, the DTP, all the standard childhood vaccines, it's very important that parents vaccinate their kids," Dr. Jay Bhattacharya, the director of the National Institutes of Health, said on "Face the Nation with Margaret Brennan." Research has found no evidence of increased rates of autism among those who are vaccinated compared to those who are not. Vaccines also undergo intensive safety testing. The CDC data indicates a decline in vaccination rates, with only 92.4% of kindergartners receiving the measles-mumps-rubella vaccine in the 2025-2026 school year, down from 92.5% the previous year.

随着疾病在多个州激增,美国 麻疹 病例已突破 4,800 例

US measles cases top 4,800 as the disease surges in multiple states
新闻简报美国麻疹病例激增,总数达到4,852例,为35年来最高的年度总数。宾夕法尼亚州是疫情中心,有 1,426 例病例,其次是威斯康星州,有 392 例病例。CDC 正在调查并提供 疫苗接种 建议,宾夕法尼亚州有 3 例确诊死亡,5 例正在接受调查。美国可能会失去 麻疹 的淘汰地位。

事件
美国的麻疹病例激增,总数达到4,852例,为35年来最高的年度总数。宾夕法尼亚州是热点地区,有 1,426 例病例,其次是威斯康星州,有 392 例病例。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

US measles cases top 4,800 as the disease surges in multiple states

News Brief
The United States has seen a surge in measles cases, with the total reaching 4,852, the highest yearly total in 35 years. Pennsylvania is the epicenter with 1,426 cases, followed by Wisconsin with 392. The CDC is investigating and providing vaccination recommendations, with 3 confirmed deaths and 5 under investigation in Pennsylvania. The US is likely to lose its measles elimination status.
Original source text (English)

Topics & Projects Antimicrobial Stewardship Chronic Wasting Disease Osterholm Update Podcasts Newsletters About CIDRAP CIDRAP in the News Our Staff Our Director Osterholm in the Press Shop Merchandise Contact Us About Search CLOSE Search Support Menu US measles cases top 4,800 as the disease surges in multiple states Jim Wappes Measles CDC / Cynthia S. Goldsmith, William Bellini Share Copied to clipboard It used to be that 200-plus US measles cases would reflect the total for the year or even for two years, but the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) today confirmed 264 new cases in just the past seven days, raising the country’s total to 4,852 cases, by far the highest yearly total in 35 years. The new data mark the 12th straight week of triple-digit increases nationwide. Rising cases in multiple states have contributed to the escalation, but Pennsylvania continues to be the nation’s hot spot, logging 88 new cases this week, followed closely by Wisconsin. The United States will very likely lose its measles elimination status—which it gained in 2000—in the coming month. Even an analysis published in May, when the country had only around 1,800 cases, said as much. The CDC has logged 43 measles outbreaks across the country so far this year, compared with 48 for all of last year, which saw 2,289 cases by year’s end. An outbreak involves three or more people. All but 18 infections this year have been locally acquired, with the rest involving international travelers. Of the confirmed cases, 17% have been in children under the age of 5 years, and 41% have been in kids and teens ages 5 to 19. Eleven percent of cases overall have required hospitalization, a number that has been slowly climbing and now matches the 2025 rate. Hospitalization rates are highest in preschoolers (16%), with a 14% rate in adults 20 and older. Though the CDC continues to acknowledge just three measles deaths this year, Pennsylvania has reported five, with a sixth death in that state under investigation. The vast majority of confirmed cases (96%) have been in people who are unvaccinated or have an unknown vaccination status. Illnesses surge in Pennsylvania, Wisconsin, Minnesota, Indiana In its ongoing outbreak, this week Pennsylvania confirmed 88 new cases and 1,426 total cases, by far the largest state outbreak the nation has seen since the early 1990s. Lancaster County continues to be the epicenter, with 493 cases now. Mifflin is second with 205 cases, followed by Chester (93) and Jefferson (88). As opposed to the national trend, 67% of reported cases in Pennsylvania have involved adults. The state’s outbreak doesn’t appear to be slowing, despite a statement from Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. two weeks ago that he will bring it “to a quick close.” An Associated Press story today, however, said more of the state’s residents are open to vaccination, including those in hard-hit Amish communities. Wisconsin saw another large jump this week, with 82 new cases and 392 total. Of those cases, 336, or 86% of the total, have been confirmed in Grant, Iowa, Lafayette, and Vernon counties in southwestern Wisconsin. State and county officials in that area “are working to identify and notify people who may have been exposed to the measles virus,” the Wisconsin Department of Health Services said. Chippewa County has 33 infections. Neighboring Minnesota has reached its highest measles total in 36 years, the Minnesota Department of Health reported yesterday. The state now has 80 cases, with 38 new cases in the past week. “The measles, mumps, and rubella vaccine is well-tested, highly effective, and provides long-lasting protection,” said Minnesota Commissioner of Health Brooke Cunningham, MD, PhD. “We strongly advise anyone who is recommended to receive the MMR vaccine and has not been vaccinated to contact their healthcare provider and get vaccinated now.” The measles, mumps, and rubella vaccine is well-tested, highly effective, and provides long-lasting protection. The disease is also surging in Indiana. With 47 newly confirmed cases, the state is just shy of 100 for the year. Today the Indiana Department of Public Health listed 97 known measles infections, with the most in LaGrange (22 cases), Adams (17), and Wayne (16) counties. Ohio confirmed 30 more infections this week, and 296 total. New York state has eight new cases, lifting its count to 170. Finally, Iowa, which before August 16 had only one case for 2026, has now recorded 42, according to Iowa Health and Human Services. Last year Iowa had nine measles cases. Cases in Kentucky have now reached 201, the state health department reported today, with four new cases this past week. Last year the state recorded only 11 measles infections. The hardest-hit counties are Christian (65 cases), Laurel (56), and Trigg (31). B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles Update: October 30, 2026

News Brief
The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) reported a 5.8% increase in measles cases in the US, bringing the total to 4,852 confirmed cases as of October 29, 2026. This is more than double the number of cases seen in all of 2025. Ninety-six percent of the cases are linked to outbreaks, and 10% of cases required hospitalization. The CDC also reported 3 measles deaths, though the total number may be higher due to probable cases not included in the official count.

Advertisement Today, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) reported there were 4,852* cases of measles in the US as of October 29, 2026. Week-over-week, this is an additional 264 cases and a 5.8% increase. Last week, the federal agency reported there were 4,588* confirmed cases.1-2 CDC said there were 4,834 measles cases were reported by 47 jurisdictions: Alabama, Alaska, Arizona, California, Colorado, Connecticut, Delaware, District of Columbia, Florida, Georgia, Idaho, Illinois, Indiana, Iowa, Kansas, Kentucky, Louisiana, Maine, Maryland, Massachusetts, Michigan, Minnesota, Missouri, Montana, Nebraska, New Jersey, New Mexico, New York City, New York State, North Carolina, North Dakota, Ohio, Oklahoma, Oregon, Pennsylvania, Rhode Island, South Carolina, South Dakota, Tennessee, Texas, Utah, Vermont, Virginia, Washington, West Virginia, Wisconsin, and Wyoming. A total of 18 measles cases were reported among international visitors to the United States. 1 The federal agency reports there have been 43 new outbreaks** reported in 2026, and 96% of confirmed cases (4,666 of 4,852) are outbreak-associated (3,282 from outbreaks starting in 2026 and 1,384 from outbreaks started in 2025).1 2026 Case Totals Already Double All of 2025 For the full year of 2025, the CDC reported 2,289 confirmed* measles cases across 45 jurisdictions, along with 48 outbreaks. This year's total of 4,852 cases is more than twice the 2025 figure. By comparison, 16 outbreaks were reported during 2024, and 69% of cases (198 of 285) were outbreak-associated.1 Measles Testing Measles PCR Testing Climbs as Positivity Rises in National Lab Data Age, Vaccination, and Hospitalization Data According to the CDC's case table, 17% of cases (766) occurred in children younger than 5 years, 42% (1914) in those aged 5 to 19 years, and 41% (1881) in adults aged 20 years or older. Overall, 96% of cases were in unvaccinated people or people with unknown vaccination status, 2% had received 1 MMR dose, and 2% had received 2 doses. Hospitalization was reported for 10% of cases (479 of 4,588), including 16% of children younger than 5 years. The CDC reports 3 measles deaths in 2026, a number it says is subject to change.1 *It is important to note the CDC says on its website the federal agency is aware of probable measles cases being reported by jurisdictions. However, the data on this page only include confirmed cases jurisdictions notified the CDC about. Therefore, there could be higher numbers in the US overall. **CDC reports the cumulative number of measles outbreaks (defined as 3 or more related cases) that have occurred this year in the US; states have the most up-to-date information about cases and outbreaks in their jurisdictions. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

回归 麻疹 消除之路由我们开始 - Healio

Road back to measles elimination starts with us - Healio
新闻简报Healio 于 2026 年 10 月 29 日发表的文章讨论了 麻疹 在美国的死灰复燃,指出今年确诊病例已逼近 5000 例,这是 33 年来的最高水平。报告强调,目前的 暴发 是自 1991 年以来最严重的一次,当时有 55,622 例病例和 123 人死亡。这篇文章强调了 疫苗 怀疑论和错误信息在推动 传播 中的作用,特别是在未接种疫苗的学龄前儿童中。它还提到了 12 个月以下儿童感染 亚急性硬化性全脑炎 (SSPE) 的可能性,这是一种严重的神经系统并发症,估计发生率为 600 人中就有 1 人。该文章提到了一个国际委员会于 2026 年 11 月评估美国是否仍然符合 麻疹 消除标准,这一状态已保持了二十多年。该文章呼吁重新关注 麻疹 和公共卫生信息,以防止进一步传播并保护儿童免受 麻疹(包括 SSPE)的长期后果。本文还涉及了关于早期 疫苗接种 时间表的争论以及平衡 疫苗 疗效与母源抗体潜在风险的挑战。响应状态涉及持续努力解决 疫苗 犹豫并改进 监测,尽管文章指出当前的方法可能会错过学校的早期 暴发。这篇文章基于 IDWeek 上发表的专家分析和参考研究,但没有提供美国和宾夕法尼亚州以外死亡人数或病例数的具体数据。
查看新闻五要素
时间
文章讨论了美国麻疹病例创下33年来新高,并提到目前的麻疹 麻疹,今年确诊病例接近5000例。它还提到 2026 年 11 月成立的一个国际委员会,以确定美国是否仍符合 麻疹 淘汰标准。[N1]、[N15]、[N18]、[N20]。
地点与对象
这篇文章重点关注美国,特别提到宾夕法尼亚州今年发生了 5 起与 麻疹 相关的死亡事件。它还提到了菲律宾,该国在 2018-2019 年 麻疹 麻疹 实施后不断出现 SSPE 案件。[N15]、[N17]、[N30]。
事件
文章报道了美国的麻疹 暴发,今年确诊病例接近5000例,仅宾夕法尼亚州就有5例死亡。它还讨论了 2018-2019 年 暴发 后菲律宾发生 SSPE 案件的可能性。
规模、影响与风险
文章指出,美国正在经历 1991 年以来最严重的 麻疹 暴发,1989 年至 1991 年间有 55,622 例病例,123 例死亡。文章还指出,估计每 600 名 12 个月以下儿童中就有 1 名感染 SSPE。[N14]、[N28]、[N29]。
应对、状态与不确定性
该文章强调了 疫苗接种 要求和快速识别案例的必要性,以防止 暴发。它还提到了平衡早期 疫苗接种 与残留母体抗体的潜在问题的挑战以及进一步研究的需要。[N32]、[N34]、[N36]、[N37]、[N38]。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Road back to measles elimination starts with us - Healio

News Brief
The article, published on October 29, 2026, by Healio, discusses the resurgence of measles in the United States, noting that confirmed cases this year are approaching 5,000, which is the highest level in 33 years. It highlights the current outbreak as the worst since 1991, when 55,622 cases and 123 deaths were recorded. The piece emphasizes the role of vaccine skepticism and misinformation in driving transmission, particularly among unvaccinated preschoolers. It also mentions the potential for subacute sclerosing panencephalitis (SSPE), a severe neurological complication, in children infected under 12 months, with an estimated occurrence rate of 1 in 600. The article references an international commission in November 2026 to assess whether the U.S. still meets measles elimination criteria, a status it has held for over two decades. The piece calls for a renewed focus on vaccination and public health messaging to prevent further spread and protect children from the long-term consequences of measles, including SSPE. The article also touches on the debate over early vaccination schedules and the challenges of balancing vaccine efficacy with potential risks of maternal antibodies. The response status involves ongoing efforts to address vaccine hesitancy and improve surveillance, though the article notes that current methods may miss early outbreaks in schools. The article is based on expert analysis and references studies presented at IDWeek, but it does not provide specific data on the number of deaths or cases beyond the U.S. and Pennsylvania.
Original source text (English)
IDWeek By Gary S. Marshall, MD Fact checked by Jennifer Mesce Purpose-built for physicians Learn more Read more November 16, 2025 5 min read What clinicians need to know as measles cases hit 33-year high November 19, 2024 4 min read Q&A: ‘We have to be worried’ about decline in measles vaccination September 18, 2025 2 min read CDC advisors table vote to delay hepatitis B vaccination in newborns October 28, 2026 2 min read Carolina schools with high vaccination rates fared better vs. measles Road back to measles elimination starts with us October 29, 2026 4 min read By Gary S. Marshall, MD Fact checked by Jennifer Mesce Add topic to email alerts Please provide your email address to receive an email when new articles are posted on Pediatric ID in Pediatrics . Click Here to Manage Email Alerts We were unable to process your request. Please try again later. If you continue to have this issue please contact customerservice@slackinc.com . I had not seen measles until 1989, when I was a fellow at The Children’s Hospital of Philadelphia. It was the beginning of the first major resurgence of measles in the United States during the vaccine era. Philadelphia went on to experience a 2-year outbreak that claimed the lives of nine children. (If you want to understand the human side of that story, read Paul Offit’s 2015 book Bad Faith .) Between 1989 and 1991, there were 55,622 measles cases and 123 associated deaths across the country. We are in the midst of the worst outbreak since then , with the number of confirmed cases this year approaching 5,000. The average number of measles-related deaths per year in the U.S. from 2000 to 2020 was less than 1; this year, there have already been 5 deaths in Pennsylvania alone . In November, an international commission will determine if the U.S. still meets criteria for measles elimination, a status it has held for over 2 decades. An analysis performed earlier this year suggests the answer is likely to be no. A main driver of measles transmission in 1991 were pockets of susceptible inner-city preschoolers with suboptimal access to healthcare. Today, a main driver is people who could have been — but were intentionally not — protected by vaccination, the cumulative effect of post-COVID vaccine skepticism, widespread misinformation and disinformation, and a federal posture that is best described as anti-scientific. If you trained in the early 2000s or thereafter, you have probably never seen the disease. That’s because measles cases plummeted after the adoption of a two-dose MMR schedule in 1989 and the implementation of school mandates. It follows, then, that you probably have not seen the consequences of measles, which, death aside, include pneumonia, encephalitis and subacute sclerosing panencephalitis (SSPE). That’s the one that really gets me. Your seemingly healthy 6-year-old develops personality change, declining school performance, myoclonus, ataxia, dysarthria, dementia and eventually coma, the result of a defective measles virus that has persisted in their central nervous system since they had an uneventful case of measles at a young age. SSPE is estimated to occur in about 1 in 600 children who are infected under 12 months. We do not know how many American children will experience this slow dying of the neurological light as a result of the current measles outbreak. In the Philippines, they are just now seeing the leading edge of a wave of SSPE cases following the measles outbreak in 2018 and 2019. A study by Lee and colleagues presented at IDWeek called attention to the importance of school as a place where outbreaks amass energy. It stands to reason that vaccination requirements (if they actually result in vaccination) and the rapid identification and exclusion of cases can squelch that energy. Recent analyses suggest that the epidemic threshold is crossed in schools years before outbreaks are apparent at the county level; this means that incipient outbreaks may be invisible to our current surveillance methods. A study by Creech and colleagues , also presented at IDWeek, begs the question of whether the first dose of MMR should be given earlier. There are trade-offs here. If we continue to wait until 12 months and the prevalence of disease increases, there will be more infant cases with their associated severity. If we vaccinate at 6 months, residual maternal antibodies could prevent “take” in a substantial proportion of infants, leaving them susceptible later on. Even in the absence of antibody interference, early vaccination could result in less perfect and shorter-lived humoral immunity. A dose at 6 months could be added to the existing 12-month and 4- to 6-year schedule, but what should be the threshold for pushing that button? In thinking about this, the cited abstracts intersect because the secondary attack rate in schools underscores that “all measles transmission is local,” and if we wait until traditional surveillance demonstrates that an outbreak is occurring, it may be too late for some infants. This conundrum would not exist if we continued along the road to sustained elimination, which could only happen if immunization rates remain high. We cannot get there by offering parents P -values and confidence intervals; instead, it will take heartfelt, impassioned appeals to the love they have for their children and a steadfast confidence on the part of their providers that vaccination is the right thing. The theory of quantum mechanics agrees with physical measurements to within 1 part per trillion. In other words, the most successful scientific theory in the history of the world might be wrong, but chances are, it’s right. This lends some perspective to the concerns that many parents have about the science behind vaccine safety. Studies show that MMR does not cause autism, to within the margin of error afforded by studies of millions of person-years of observation. Just like proofs of quantum mechanics, there will always be some margin of error. The question comes down to balancing the incredibly small probability of error with the very real threat to health and well-being that measles represents. For more information: Gary S. Marshall, MD, is a professor of pediatrics and chief of the division of pediatric infectious diseases at Norton Children’s and the University of Louisville School of Medicine. He is the author of Healio’s Clinical Guidance module on measles and The Vaccine Handbook . He can be reached at pids@idsociety.org .
